    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nPractitioners monitoring iterative training pipelines face a diagnostic blind spot: the metrics most commonly used to detect distributional degradation can yield false positive rates of 62.5--100\\% when applied without proper statistical controls, yet no established protocol exists for distinguishing genuine early-warning signals from artifacts of shared non-stationary trends.\nThis blind spot is consequential because iterative training collapse, where models are unwittingly trained on output from earlier generations, is increasingly likely as machine-generated text proliferates on the web~\\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nTail modes vanish, lexical diversity narrows, and calibration deteriorates across generations.\nThese failure modes motivate the search for cheap-to-compute \\emph{canary metrics} that provide early warning, analogous to early-warning signals for critical transitions in ecological and climate systems~\\cite{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\n\nSeveral recent studies have pursued this direction: \\citet{du2026geometric} informally observe that expected calibration error rises before accuracy degrades; SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} proposes spectral indicators of distributional shift; and DCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal.\nYet the statistical methodology underlying these lead-lag claims (whether a canary metric genuinely \\emph{precedes} downstream degradation, or merely correlates with it under shared non-stationary trends) has received little scrutiny.\n\nWe identify several methodological deficiencies that pervade existing lead-lag analyses of iterative training collapse:\n(1)~no differencing tests to rule out spurious regression arising from shared non-stationary trends, a well-known hazard when correlating co-trending time series;\n(2)~no multi-method cross-validation; conclusions rest on a single statistical method whose implicit assumptions about stationarity and directionality are rarely examined;\n(3)~no multiple comparison correction (e.g., Benjamini--Hochberg FDR control), increasing the risk of false discoveries when testing many metric pairs simultaneously; and\n(4)~no regime-dependent analysis, despite the possibility that the same statistical test yields opposite conclusions at different contamination levels.\nThe closest predecessor is \\citet{du2026geometric}, who observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models.\nTheir analysis, however, is limited to a single metric pair, spans only 1--3 generations, and reports informal visual comparisons without statistical tests---leaving open the question of whether the observed lead is genuine or an artifact of shared trends.\nThe early-warning framework of \\citet{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}---widely adopted in ecology for anticipating tipping points---provides the theoretical motivation for canary-based prediction, but has not been systematically applied to iterative training collapse with the methodological rigor that short, noisy time series demand~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\n\nWe address these gaps through a systematic case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse---the progressive loss of lexical diversity in model-generated text across training generations.\nUsing Pythia-410M across five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$) and up to four random seeds, we expose three methodological pitfalls that undermine naive lead-lag analysis:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Method sensitivity.} Applied to the same data (16 candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, including candidates later excluded; \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (poor agreement).\n    \\item \\textbf{Spurious regression.} Canary$\\to$perplexity associations appear near-universal in undifferenced analysis but vanish entirely after first-differencing ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), revealing them as artifacts of co-trending rather than genuine temporal precedence.\n    \\item \\textbf{Regime dependence.} Permutation Granger tests find 24/24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (0/8--6/8, three seeds), demonstrating that detectability depends sharply on both contamination level and training stochasticity.\n\\end{enumerate}\nTo move beyond single-method analysis, we propose a multi-method consensus framework with empirically calibrated thresholds, requiring agreement across multiple independent statistical methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\nThe framework's utility is demonstrated by its calibration on built-in experimental controls.\nNaive cross-correlation identifies 100\\% of canary--collapse metric pairs as FDR-significant; the consensus pipeline (requiring ${\\geq}4/5$ methods and ${\\geq}3/4$ seeds) retains only those pairs meeting effect-size criteria calibrated against the null control.\nOn reference extremes, the framework achieves a false positive rate of 0\\% at the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (0/24 pairs, three seeds) (where individual methods reach up to 100\\% FPR) and a false negative rate of 0\\% against six effect-size reference pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nApplying the framework, we identify token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$: the full consensus pipeline confirms directional asymmetry with cross-correlation $|r|{>}0.99$, 24/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant, and 0/24 in the reverse direction.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ extends this finding (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse via permutation Granger), though method-level consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3).\nThe onset boundary of causal detectability lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, below which all seeds agree on null.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic exposure of three methodological pitfalls} (method sensitivity, spurious regression, regime dependence) in lead-lag analysis of iterative training collapse.\n    Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%; first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely; and the same Granger test shifts from 24/24 FDR-significant pairs to 0/8 across contamination levels.\n    These results demonstrate that single-method, single-regime analyses---the current norm in this literature---are unreliable.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\\%).\n    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.\n    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture and cross-domain generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): evaluation of the consensus pipeline across model scale (Pythia-1B), architecture family (OPT-1.3B), and training domain (C4), testing whether the methodological pitfalls and detectability boundaries identified on Pythia-410M / WikiText-103 transfer to other settings. \\textbf{[Results TBD.]}\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys related work on model collapse, early-warning signals, and lead-lag methodology.\n\\S\\ref{sec:background} establishes the formal setup, notation, and the five detection methods used throughout.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} distills the three methodological pitfalls into a unified diagnostic framework, presents the multi-method consensus pipeline, and reports its empirical calibration.\n\\S\\ref{sec:experiments} provides full experimental results: method sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), regime-dependent detectability (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), a case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), consensus framework validation (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}), and cross-architecture generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications---including the practical utility paradox, the token entropy--perplexity distinction, and NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis---and outlines directions for future work.\n\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes, followed by a dedicated Limitations section (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).",

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "ca96d8df-f4b7-45ad-89b2-cc87d4a0a5dc",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "aa1c9749-5d07-40b8-abfb-a7eed1a4f660",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "27ba8eb1-a0ba-4ef1-b625-3a975237650e",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "diversity collapse---the progressive loss",
    "newString": "diversity collapse, the progressive loss",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nPractitioners monitoring iterative training pipelines face a diagnostic blind spot: the metrics most commonly used to detect distributional degradation can yield false positive rates of 62.5--100\\% when applied without proper statistical controls, yet no established protocol exists for distinguishing genuine early-warning signals from artifacts of shared non-stationary trends.\nThis blind spot is consequential because iterative training collapse, where models are unwittingly trained on output from earlier generations, is increasingly likely as machine-generated text proliferates on the web~\\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nTail modes vanish, lexical diversity narrows, and calibration deteriorates across generations.\nThese failure modes motivate the search for cheap-to-compute \\emph{canary metrics} that provide early warning, analogous to early-warning signals for critical transitions in ecological and climate systems~\\cite{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\n\nSeveral recent studies have pursued this direction: \\citet{du2026geometric} informally observe that expected calibration error rises before accuracy degrades; SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} proposes spectral indicators of distributional shift; and DCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal.\nYet the statistical methodology underlying these lead-lag claims (whether a canary metric genuinely \\emph{precedes} downstream degradation, or merely correlates with it under shared non-stationary trends) has received little scrutiny.\n\nWe identify several methodological deficiencies that pervade existing lead-lag analyses of iterative training collapse:\n(1)~no differencing tests to rule out spurious regression arising from shared non-stationary trends, a well-known hazard when correlating co-trending time series;\n(2)~no multi-method cross-validation; conclusions rest on a single statistical method whose implicit assumptions about stationarity and directionality are rarely examined;\n(3)~no multiple comparison correction (e.g., Benjamini--Hochberg FDR control), increasing the risk of false discoveries when testing many metric pairs simultaneously; and\n(4)~no regime-dependent analysis, despite the possibility that the same statistical test yields opposite conclusions at different contamination levels.\nThe closest predecessor is \\citet{du2026geometric}, who observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models.\nTheir analysis, however, is limited to a single metric pair, spans only 1--3 generations, and reports informal visual comparisons without statistical tests, leaving open the question of whether the observed lead is genuine or an artifact of shared trends.\nThe early-warning framework of \\citet{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}, widely adopted in ecology for anticipating tipping points, provides the theoretical motivation for canary-based prediction, but has not been systematically applied to iterative training collapse with the methodological rigor that short, noisy time series demand~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\n\nWe address these gaps through a systematic case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse---the progressive loss of lexical diversity in model-generated text across training generations.\nUsing Pythia-410M across five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$) and up to four random seeds, we expose three methodological pitfalls that undermine naive lead-lag analysis:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Method sensitivity.} Applied to the same data (16 candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, including candidates later excluded; \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (poor agreement).\n    \\item \\textbf{Spurious regression.} Canary$\\to$perplexity associations appear near-universal in undifferenced analysis but vanish entirely after first-differencing ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), revealing them as artifacts of co-trending rather than genuine temporal precedence.\n    \\item \\textbf{Regime dependence.} Permutation Granger tests find 24/24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (0/8--6/8, three seeds), demonstrating that detectability depends sharply on both contamination level and training stochasticity.\n\\end{enumerate}\nTo move beyond single-method analysis, we propose a multi-method consensus framework with empirically calibrated thresholds, requiring agreement across multiple independent statistical methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\nThe framework's utility is demonstrated by its calibration on built-in experimental controls.\nNaive cross-correlation identifies 100\\% of canary--collapse metric pairs as FDR-significant; the consensus pipeline (requiring ${\\geq}4/5$ methods and ${\\geq}3/4$ seeds) retains only those pairs meeting effect-size criteria calibrated against the null control.\nOn reference extremes, the framework achieves a false positive rate of 0\\% at the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (0/24 pairs, three seeds) (where individual methods reach up to 100\\% FPR) and a false negative rate of 0\\% against six effect-size reference pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nApplying the framework, we identify token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$: the full consensus pipeline confirms directional asymmetry with cross-correlation $|r|{>}0.99$, 24/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant, and 0/24 in the reverse direction.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ extends this finding (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse via permutation Granger), though method-level consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3).\nThe onset boundary of causal detectability lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, below which all seeds agree on null.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic exposure of three methodological pitfalls} (method sensitivity, spurious regression, regime dependence) in lead-lag analysis of iterative training collapse.\n    Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%; first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely; and the same Granger test shifts from 24/24 FDR-significant pairs to 0/8 across contamination levels.\n    These results demonstrate that single-method, single-regime analyses---the current norm in this literature---are unreliable.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\\%).\n    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.\n    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture and cross-domain generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): evaluation of the consensus pipeline across model scale (Pythia-1B), architecture family (OPT-1.3B), and training domain (C4), testing whether the methodological pitfalls and detectability boundaries identified on Pythia-410M / WikiText-103 transfer to other settings. \\textbf{[Results TBD.]}\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys related work on model collapse, early-warning signals, and lead-lag methodology.\n\\S\\ref{sec:background} establishes the formal setup, notation, and the five detection methods used throughout.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} distills the three methodological pitfalls into a unified diagnostic framework, presents the multi-method consensus pipeline, and reports its empirical calibration.\n\\S\\ref{sec:experiments} provides full experimental results: method sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), regime-dependent detectability (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), a case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), consensus framework validation (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}), and cross-architecture generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications---including the practical utility paradox, the token entropy--perplexity distinction, and NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis---and outlines directions for future work.\n\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes, followed by a dedicated Limitations section (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).",

          "-    \\item[(a)] The canary$\\to$downstream drift ratio at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ must exceed $3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline drift (canary ratios: entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$---both far above threshold); and",
          "+    \\item[(a)] The canary$\\to$downstream drift ratio at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ must exceed $3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline drift (canary ratios: entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$, both far above threshold); and",
          "     \\item[(b)] Canary onset must strictly precede downstream onset by at least one generation (canary onset: generation~1; diversity onset: generations~3--9).",
          " \\end{enumerate}",
          " These criteria yield token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1, distinct-2, and distinct-3~\\cite{li2016diversity}."

          " The fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \\textbf{16/16} (100\\%), differenced cross-correlation \\textbf{14/16} (87.5\\%), threshold onset detection \\textbf{9/16} (56.3\\%), permutation Granger causality \\textbf{6/16} (37.5\\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \\textbf{4/16} (25.0\\%).",

          "+The spurious-signal rate (the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative) is \\textbf{75\\%} across all five methods and \\textbf{62.5\\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).",
          " Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.",

          " At $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds), permutation Granger finds 19/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant with 0/24 reverse, confirming directional asymmetry; however, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds), placing this regime in a transitional detection state.",
          "-Under Bonferroni correction, approximately 3/8 pairs per seed survive---consistent with the transitional nature of the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ boundary where marginal signals are sensitive to correction stringency.",
          "+Under Bonferroni correction, approximately 3/8 pairs per seed survive, consistent with the transitional nature of the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ boundary where marginal signals are sensitive to correction stringency.",

          " The resulting dose-response pattern is monotonic: causal detectability is absent below $\\alpha{=}0.25$, seed-dependent at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, and robust above $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).",
          " The onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HLFc1ye7ntUMQdVjWrwnHx`</summary>

```text
23	\paragraph{Why $K{=}4/5$ is the consensus tipping point.}
24	The $K{=}4/5$ threshold reflects the composition of the five-method panel rather than an arbitrary choice.
25	At $K{=}3/5$, the two high-FPR methods (naive cross-correlation at 100\% and differenced cross-correlation at 37.5\%) can form a majority with any single additional method, allowing false positives to pass even when both conservative methods (permutation Granger and Toda--Yamamoto) disagree.
26	At $K{=}4/5$, any agreeing coalition must include at least one low-FPR method, which effectively vetoes spurious signals: null-control FPR drops from 37.5\% to 0\%.
27	Raising the threshold to $K{=}5/5$ requires Toda--Yamamoto agreement, but its low power at $T{=}10$ produces Type~II errors on 2/6 reference pairs (FNR${=}$50\%).
28	The $K{=}4/5$ threshold thus occupies the unique operating point where exactly one low-power method can be excluded without admitting high-FPR coalitions.
29	
30	\paragraph{Short time-series constraint.}
31	With $T{=}10$ generation cycles (11 data points per series), our time-series analyses operate near the lower bound of what standard methods require.
32	This series length is typical for iterative training studies, where each generation involves a complete training run, but it severely limits statistical power.
33	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure's elevated FNR (33.3\%, missing 2 of 6 directional pairs) is attributable to insufficient residual degrees of freedom at this series length, not to a fundamental deficiency in the method itself.
34	This finding aligns with \citet{boettiger2012limits}, who demonstrated that early-warning signals have limited detection power in short time series.
35	Longer generation chains would improve power for all methods but impose substantial computational cost; this trade-off between statistical power and compute budget is inherent to iterative training research and cannot be resolved by methodological improvements alone.
36	However, observation window sensitivity analysis provides reassurance: the $4/5$ consensus classification stabilizes at $T{\geq}5$, well below our observation window of $T{=}10$, with identical results from $T{=}5$ through $T{=}10$ (Appendix~\ref{app:window_sensitivity}).
37	The Toda--Yamamoto procedure produces valid test statistics at all window lengths ($T \in \{5, \ldots, 10\}$), but with unstable sensitivity (3/6 to 6/6 pairs reach significance depending on window length), confirming that its elevated FNR reflects limited residual degrees of freedom rather than absence of signal.
38	
39	\paragraph{Effective rank direction is model-dependent.}
40	Effective rank evolves in opposite directions across architectures during iterative training.
41	In Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases (representational contraction), consistent with mode collapse concentrating the output distribution into fewer dimensions.
42	In GPT-2-medium at $\alpha{=}0.25$, effective rank \emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\%).
43	The likely explanation is starting-point dependence: GPT-2-medium begins near rank-2 (a highly concentrated initial representation), and iterative training disperses activation mass across more dimensions rather than concentrating it further.
44	This finding demonstrates that effective rank cannot serve as a direction-agnostic canary and that its trajectory must be interpreted relative to architecture-specific baselines.
45	We note this as a caveat on the generality of representational metrics as collapse indicators, even when the regime map (Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime}) and lead-lag detectability are consistent across architectures.
46	
47	\paragraph{Diversity--calibration dissociation.}
48	Diversity metrics and calibration metrics respond to synthetic contamination in qualitatively opposite directions across the $\alpha$ spectrum.
49	Diversity collapse (distinct-$n$ decline) is most severe at low contamination ($\alpha{=}0.25$: distinct-3 declines by $58.9{\pm}0.9\%$) and attenuates at higher levels ($\alpha{=}1.0$: $-30.5\%$), while calibration degradation (ECE) increases monotonically with $\alpha$ ($+51.7\%$ at $\alpha{=}0.0$ to $+3780\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
50	This dissociation underscores the need for multi-method consensus: the regime in which a metric degrades most severely ($\alpha{=}0.25$ for diversity) does not coincide with the regime in which its temporal lead is most detectable ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), and a single metric family cannot capture the full picture of iterative training degradation.
51	
52	\paragraph{Practical utility paradox.}
53	The canary framework exhibits a paradox: it works best where it is least needed.
54	At $\alpha{\geq}0.75$, token entropy reliably Granger-precedes diversity collapse, but at these contamination levels degradation is already severe and likely detectable through simpler means.
55	At $\alpha{\leq}0.25$, where early warning would be most valuable, the canary signal is undetectable by any method tested; at $\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8), marking the onset boundary where ECE outperforms token entropy as the more sensitive canary.
56	This sensitivity difference reflects how each metric aggregates distributional information: ECE decomposes calibration error across multiple probability bins, making it responsive to changes in the shape of the predicted distribution rather than just its mean, while token entropy compresses the full distribution into a single scalar average.
57	At moderate contamination ($\alpha{=}0.50$), the distributional shifts are subtle enough that bin-level decomposition detects them but the global average does not.
58	This is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\cite{boettiger2012limits}.
59	The practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards that track cumulative trend shifts across many generations than to single-point threshold alarms.
60	If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.
61	
62	\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}
63	The consensus framework can function as an automated quality gate for iterative training pipelines, analogous to how unit tests guard against software regressions.
64	In a production setting, the multi-method panel would run after each generation, and any metric pair crossing the Tier~3 threshold would trigger a review of the training data composition.
65	This shifts the framework's role from post-hoc forensic analysis to prospective pipeline health monitoring, where the cost of running five lightweight statistical tests per generation is negligible relative to the cost of training itself.
66	
67	\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}
```

probability bins, making it responsive to changes in the shape of the predicted distribution rather than just its mean, while token entropy compresses the full distribution into a single scalar average.\nAt moderate contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), the distributional shifts are subtle enough that bin-level decomposition detects them but the global average does not.\nThis is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nThe practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards that track cumulative trend shifts across many generations than to single-point threshold alarms.\nIf contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.\n\n\\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}\nThe consensus framework can function as an automated quality gate for iterative training pipelines, analogous to how unit tests guard against software regressions.\nIn a production setting, the multi-method panel would run after each generation, and any metric pair crossing the Tier~3 threshold would trigger a review of the training data composition.\nThis shifts the framework's role from post-hoc forensic analysis to prospective pipeline health monitoring, where the cost of running five lightweight statistical tests per generation is negligible relative to the cost of training itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Token entropy versus perplexity.}",
      "numLines": 45,
      "startLine": 23,
      "totalLines": 90
    }
  },

{

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}\nThe consensus framework can function as an automated quality gate for iterative training pipelines, analogous to how unit tests guard against software regressions.\nIn a production setting, the multi-method panel would run after each generation, and any metric pair crossing the Tier~3 threshold would trigger a review of the training data composition.\nThis shifts the framework's role from post-hoc forensic analysis to prospective pipeline health monitoring, where the cost of running five lightweight statistical tests per generation is negligible relative to the cost of training itself.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}\nIn the Confirmatory regime ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$), the consensus framework can serve as a continuous monitoring signal within iterative training pipelines: the multi-method panel would run after each generation, and sustained Tier~3 agreement across metric pairs would flag potential collapse onset for human review.\nThis complements the continuous monitoring dashboard framing above, where cumulative trend shifts across generations provide more reliable detection than single-point threshold alarms.\nThe cost of running five lightweight statistical tests per generation is negligible relative to the cost of training itself, though the practical utility paradox (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}) and the absence of intervention experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}) mean that such monitoring currently provides diagnostic evidence rather than actionable triggers."
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KEnvp643oCk5x7bgh8Y5qq`</summary>

```text
58	This is not a framework deficiency but an inherent characteristic of collapse dynamics: at low contamination, diversity changes are too small relative to inter-generation noise for any temporal lead to emerge above the statistical detection floor~\cite{boettiger2012limits}.
59	The practical implication is that the framework is better suited to continuous monitoring dashboards that track cumulative trend shifts across many generations than to single-point threshold alarms.
60	If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.
61	
62	\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}
63	In the Confirmatory regime ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$), the consensus framework can serve as a continuous monitoring signal within iterative training pipelines: the multi-method panel would run after each generation, and sustained Tier~3 agreement across metric pairs would flag potential collapse onset for human review.
64	This complements the continuous monitoring dashboard framing above, where cumulative trend shifts across generations provide more reliable detection than single-point threshold alarms.
65	The cost of running five lightweight statistical tests per generation is negligible relative to the cost of training itself, though the practical utility paradox (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) and the absence of intervention experiments (\S\ref{sec:limitations}) mean that such monitoring currently provides diagnostic evidence rather than actionable triggers.
```

</details>

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "6941310a-4a15-4d2f-a349-ccd88c4c6297",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "f93193b7-4eff-425c-a4cf-35d0a51c8d58",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "7c04edde-0e35-4dee-91e5-24d97d71e749",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture and cross-domain generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): evaluation of the consensus pipeline across model scale (Pythia-1B), architecture family (OPT-1.3B), and training domain (C4), testing whether the methodological pitfalls and detectability boundaries identified on Pythia-410M / WikiText-103 transfer to other settings. \\textbf{[Results TBD.]}",
    "newString": "    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): validation on GPT-2-medium (345M parameters) confirms that the entropy-leads-diversity pattern, the consensus framework's operating thresholds, and the detectability boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ all transfer across decoder-only transformer families, with all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reaching Tier~3 consensus at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ and 0/6 in the reverse direction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nPractitioners monitoring iterative training pipelines face a diagnostic blind spot: the metrics most commonly used to detect distributional degradation can yield false positive rates of 62.5--100\\% when applied without proper statistical controls, yet no established protocol exists for distinguishing genuine early-warning signals from artifacts of shared non-stationary trends.\nThis blind spot is consequential because iterative training collapse, where models are unwittingly trained on output from earlier generations, is increasingly likely as machine-generated text proliferates on the web~\\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nTail modes vanish, lexical diversity narrows, and calibration deteriorates across generations.\nThese failure modes motivate the search for cheap-to-compute \\emph{canary metrics} that provide early warning, analogous to early-warning signals for critical transitions in ecological and climate systems~\\cite{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\n\nSeveral recent studies have pursued this direction: \\citet{du2026geometric} informally observe that expected calibration error rises before accuracy degrades; SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} proposes spectral indicators of distributional shift; and DCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal.\nYet the statistical methodology underlying these lead-lag claims (whether a canary metric genuinely \\emph{precedes} downstream degradation, or merely correlates with it under shared non-stationary trends) has received little scrutiny.\n\nWe identify several methodological deficiencies that pervade existing lead-lag analyses of iterative training collapse:\n(1)~no differencing tests to rule out spurious regression arising from shared non-stationary trends, a well-known hazard when correlating co-trending time series;\n(2)~no multi-method cross-validation; conclusions rest on a single statistical method whose implicit assumptions about stationarity and directionality are rarely examined;\n(3)~no multiple comparison correction (e.g., Benjamini--Hochberg FDR control), increasing the risk of false discoveries when testing many metric pairs simultaneously; and\n(4)~no regime-dependent analysis, despite the possibility that the same statistical test yields opposite conclusions at different contamination levels.\nThe closest predecessor is \\citet{du2026geometric}, who observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models.\nTheir analysis, however, is limited to a single metric pair, spans only 1--3 generations, and reports informal visual comparisons without statistical tests, leaving open the question of whether the observed lead is genuine or an artifact of shared trends.\nThe early-warning framework of \\citet{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}, widely adopted in ecology for anticipating tipping points, provides the theoretical motivation for canary-based prediction, but has not been systematically applied to iterative training collapse with the methodological rigor that short, noisy time series demand~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\n\nWe address these gaps through a systematic case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse, the progressive loss of lexical diversity in model-generated text across training generations.\nUsing Pythia-410M across five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$) and up to four random seeds, we expose three methodological pitfalls that undermine naive lead-lag analysis:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Method sensitivity.} Applied to the same data (16 candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, including candidates later excluded; \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (poor agreement).\n    \\item \\textbf{Spurious regression.} Canary$\\to$perplexity associations appear near-universal in undifferenced analysis but vanish entirely after first-differencing ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), revealing them as artifacts of co-trending rather than genuine temporal precedence.\n    \\item \\textbf{Regime dependence.} Permutation Granger tests find 24/24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (0/8--6/8, three seeds), demonstrating that detectability depends sharply on both contamination level and training stochasticity.\n\\end{enumerate}\nTo move beyond single-method analysis, we propose a multi-method consensus framework with empirically calibrated thresholds, requiring agreement across multiple independent statistical methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\nThe framework's utility is demonstrated by its calibration on built-in experimental controls.\nNaive cross-correlation identifies 100\\% of canary--collapse metric pairs as FDR-significant; the consensus pipeline (requiring ${\\geq}4/5$ methods and ${\\geq}3/4$ seeds) retains only those pairs meeting effect-size criteria calibrated against the null control.\nOn reference extremes, the framework achieves a false positive rate of 0\\% at the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (0/24 pairs, three seeds) (where individual methods reach up to 100\\% FPR) and a false negative rate of 0\\% against six effect-size reference pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nApplying the framework, we identify token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$: the full consensus pipeline confirms directional asymmetry with cross-correlation $|r|{>}0.99$, 24/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant, and 0/24 in the reverse direction.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ extends this finding (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse via permutation Granger), though method-level consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3).\nThe onset boundary of causal detectability lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, below which all seeds agree on null.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic exposure of three methodological pitfalls} (method sensitivity, spurious regression, regime dependence) in lead-lag analysis of iterative training collapse.\n    Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%; first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely; and the same Granger test shifts from 24/24 FDR-significant pairs to 0/8 across contamination levels.\n    These results demonstrate that single-method, single-regime analyses, the current norm in this literature, are unreliable.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\\%).\n    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.\n    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture and cross-domain generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): evaluation of the consensus pipeline across model scale (Pythia-1B), architecture family (OPT-1.3B), and training domain (C4), testing whether the methodological pitfalls and detectability boundaries identified on Pythia-410M / WikiText-103 transfer to other settings. \\textbf{[Results TBD.]}\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys related work on model collapse, early-warning signals, and lead-lag methodology.\n\\S\\ref{sec:background} establishes the formal setup, notation, and the five detection methods used throughout.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} distills the three methodological pitfalls into a unified diagnostic framework, presents the multi-method consensus pipeline, and reports its empirical calibration.\n\\S\\ref{sec:experiments} provides full experimental results: method sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), regime-dependent detectability (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), a case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), consensus framework validation (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}), and cross-architecture generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications (including the practical utility paradox, the token entropy--perplexity distinction, and NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis) and outlines directions for future work.\n\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes, followed by a dedicated Limitations section (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).",

          " ",
          "     \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.",

  "old_string": "If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.\n\n\\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}",
  "new_string": "If contamination grows over time (as in realistic web-scale data pipelines), the canary signal will strengthen as $\\alpha$ increases, providing earlier detection than post-hoc quality audits.\n\n\\paragraph{Why this matters despite the paradox.}\nIn practice, the effective contamination level $\\alpha$ is unknown and typically increases over time as web-scraped training corpora accumulate more machine-generated text.\nA continuous monitoring dashboard would automatically trigger when $\\alpha$ crosses the detectability threshold ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.50$), converting the paradox from a static limitation into a temporal advantage: the framework detects collapse precisely when contamination first becomes severe enough to matter.\nThe methodological contribution is independent of canary utility: the consensus framework and its three-pitfall diagnostic apply to any short-time-series lead-lag analysis in iterative training, regardless of which specific metrics are monitored.\nThe regime map itself is an actionable output: practitioners who know that $\\alpha{<}0.25$ is below the detection floor should invest in data provenance and source filtering rather than metric monitoring at those contamination levels.\n\n\\paragraph{Broader implications for training pipelines.}"
}

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] 整合两个新的论文可视化/表格 + 修正 method.tex Shapley 数字。\n\n**1. 新 Figure: Shapley Method Contribution**\n- PDF 位于远程 `docs/paper/figures/shapley_method_contribution.pdf`（Agent 已生成）\n- 放入 experiments.tex 或 method.tex 的 \"Method Sensitivity\" 小节（§3.3 或 §4.3，看哪个更合适）\n- Caption 要点：真 Shapley values（32 coalition 枚举），K=⌈0.8|S|⌉ 阈值，FPR 基于 α=0.0 × 3 seeds × 24 pairs，FNR 基于 α=1.0 × 4 seeds × 6 reference pairs\n- 核心叙事：Naive XCorr 是 FPR 唯一正贡献者（+0.192），PermG 是最强 FPR 抑制器（−0.110），TY 对 sensitivity 贡献弱于其他方法（−0.033 vs −0.242）\n- Figure ref 需要在正文中引用\n\n**2. 新 Table: Single-Method Error Consequences**\n- LaTeX 在远程 `docs/paper/tables/single_method_errors.tex`\n- 放入 experiments.tex 的 consensus validation 部分或 method.tex §3.3\n- Caption：展示如果只用单个方法会在每个 α 下犯什么错误（FP/FN/correct）\n- 核心叙事：Naive XCorr 在 null control 下 100% FP；TY 在 α=0.75 下 FN；consensus K≥4/5 在所有 regime 均正确\n\n**3. 修正 method.tex Shapley 数字**\n- 当前 method.tex 引用 \"PermG Shapley = +0.375, TY = −2.625\" — 这是 LOO 值不是真 Shapley\n- 更新为真 Shapley：PermG Shapley(FPR) = −0.110（降低 FPR），TY Shapley(FNR) = −0.033（对 sensitivity 贡献弱）\n- 确保叙事方向不变：PermG 仍是\"最有价值的 FP 抑制器\"，TY 仍是\"对 consensus 贡献最弱\"\n- 如果原文的叙事逻辑依赖具体数值的正负号，注意 LOO 的 +0.375（ΔFPR when removed = FPR 升高）vs Shapley 的 −0.110（加入后 FPR 降低）方向相反但含义一致\n\n**约束**：\n- 不要引入新 em-dash\n- 维持 23 页内（新 figure/table 可能需要调整 placement 或使用 appendix）\n- 这次不用验证引用"

67	\paragraph{Practical guidance.}
68	The consensus thresholds $(K, S)$ should be chosen to match the analyst's tolerance for false positives versus false negatives.
69	Table~\ref{tab:practical_guidance} provides four recommended tiers, calibrated against our experimental controls (\S\ref{sec:calibration}).
70	Tier~3 ($K{\geq}4/5$, $S{\geq}3/4$) achieves the best trade-off in our setting: zero false positives while recovering all six reference pairs.
71	Method subset ablation confirms that this threshold is uniquely optimal: at $K{=}3/5$, the null-control FPR is 37.5\% (unacceptable); at $K{=}4/5$, FPR drops to 0\% while retaining 6/6 reference-pair sensitivity; at $K{=}5/5$, FPR remains 0\% but sensitivity falls to 3/6.
72	Shapley value analysis identifies permutation Granger causality as the linchpin method (Shapley $= +0.375$), the only method combining 0\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.
73	Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively (Shapley $= -2.625$) due to its Type~II errors at $T{=}10$; we retain it as insurance against unknown integration order in our consensus panel, but note its severe power limitations at $T{\leq}10$ and caution that its results should be interpreted accordingly.
74	Tier~4 requires unanimity across methods and seeds, which incurs a 50\% false negative rate because Toda--Yamamoto produces Type~II errors at $T{=}10$ due to insufficient residual degrees of freedom (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
75	
76	\begin{table}[t]

```text
1	# Leave-One-Out Method Contribution Analysis
2	
3	## Setup
4	- Consensus rule: K>=3 out of 5 methods (full), K>=3 out of 4 (LOO)
5	- FPR computed at α=0.0 (null, 2 seeds × 8 pairs = 16 obs)
6	- FNR computed at α=1.0 (4 seeds × 8 pairs, cross-seed aggregated = 8 obs)
7	- Baseline: FPR=25.0%, FNR=0.0%
8	
9	## Results
10	
11	| Method           | ΔFPR     | ΔFNR     | Interpretation                        |
12	|-----------------|----------|----------|---------------------------------------|
13	| Naive XCorr     | -25.0%   | +25.0%   | Major FP driver, essential for sensitivity |
14	| Diff XCorr      | -25.0%   | +25.0%   | Major FP driver, essential for sensitivity |
15	| Threshold Onset | -6.2%    | +25.0%   | Minor FP contributor, essential for sensitivity |
16	| Perm Granger    | -18.8%   | 0.0%     | Pure FP filter, no sensitivity cost   |
17	| TY Granger      | 0.0%     | 0.0%     | Zero marginal contribution            |
18	
19	## Key Findings
20	1. **Naive XCorr and Diff XCorr** are "double-edged": they drive both correct detections and false positives equally
21	2. **Threshold Onset** is primarily a sensitivity contributor with minimal FPR contribution (-6.2%)
22	3. **Perm Granger** acts as a pure false-positive filter: removing it reduces FPR by 18.8% without affecting sensitivity
23	4. **TY Granger** has zero marginal effect on consensus decisions (redundant with other methods)
24	5. The three correlation-based methods (naive, diff, threshold) are all individually essential for FNR=0% at α=1.0
25	
26	## Figure Location
27	- Server: `docs/paper/figures/shapley_method_contribution.pdf` (and .png)
28	- Data source: `results/method_ablation_phase2_panel.json` + individual `5method_leadlag.json` files
29	
```

43	
44	    \item \textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\%).
45	    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.
46	    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.
47

238	Toda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\% (2/6), missing ECE$\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.
239	Both FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\times}$seed dimension (FPR: 3~seeds at $\alpha{=}0.0$; FNR: 4~seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$), with cross-seed FPR variance negligible (all three seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\geq}4/5$).
240	Table~\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.
241	
242	\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}
243	To quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
244	Permutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.
245	Toda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.
246	We note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\beta{=}0.3$).
247	
248	\begin{table}[t]
249	\centering

  "old_string": "Shapley value analysis identifies permutation Granger causality as the linchpin method (Shapley $= +0.375$), the only method combining 0\\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.\nToda--Yamamoto contributes negatively (Shapley $= -2.625$) due to its Type~II errors at $T{=}10$; we retain it as insurance against unknown integration order in our consensus panel, but note its severe power limitations at $T{\\leq}10$ and caution that its results should be interpreted accordingly.",
  "new_string": "Shapley value analysis (exact enumeration of all 32 coalitions; Figure~\\ref{fig:shapley}) identifies naive cross-correlation as the sole positive contributor to FPR (Shapley${=}{+}0.192$) and permutation Granger as the strongest FPR suppressor (Shapley${=}{-}0.110$), the only method combining 0\\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.\nToda--Yamamoto's contribution to sensitivity is the weakest in the panel (Shapley${=}{-}0.033$ vs.\\ ${-}0.242$ for the next weakest method); we retain it as insurance against unknown integration order, but note its severe power limitations at $T{\\leq}10$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/shapley_method_contribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Shapley value decomposition of method contributions to consensus FPR and FNR (exact enumeration of all $2^5{=}32$ coalitions, $K{=}\\lceil 0.8|S|\\rceil$ threshold). FPR is computed at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (3~seeds $\\times$ 8~pairs${=}$24 observations); FNR at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, 6 reference pairs). Naive cross-correlation is the only positive FPR contributor (${+}0.192$); permutation Granger is the strongest FPR suppressor (${-}0.110$). Toda--Yamamoto contributes the least to sensitivity (${-}0.033$), consistent with its low power at $T{=}10$.}\n\\label{fig:shapley}\n\\end{figure}"
}

          "+Shapley value analysis (exact enumeration of all 32 coalitions; Figure~\\ref{fig:shapley}) identifies naive cross-correlation as the sole positive contributor to FPR (Shapley${=}{+}0.192$) and permutation Granger as the strongest FPR suppressor (Shapley${=}{-}0.110$), the only method combining 0\\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.",

FPR is computed at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (3~seeds $\\times$ 8~pairs${=}$24 observations); FNR at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, 6 reference pairs).     "newString": "    Shapley-value decomposition (exact 32-coalition enumeration) reveals that naive cross-correlation is the sole FPR driver (Shapley${=}{+}0.192$) while permutation Granger is the strongest FPR suppressor (Shapley${=}{-}0.110$); these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nPractitioners monitoring iterative training pipelines face a diagnostic blind spot: the metrics most commonly used to detect distributional degradation can yield false positive rates of 62.5--100\\% when applied without proper statistical controls, yet no established protocol exists for distinguishing genuine early-warning signals from artifacts of shared non-stationary trends.\nThis blind spot is consequential because iterative training collapse, where models are unwittingly trained on output from earlier generations, is increasingly likely as machine-generated text proliferates on the web~\\cite{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nTail modes vanish, lexical diversity narrows, and calibration deteriorates across generations.\nThese failure modes motivate the search for cheap-to-compute \\emph{canary metrics} that provide early warning, analogous to early-warning signals for critical transitions in ecological and climate systems~\\cite{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}.\n\nSeveral recent studies have pursued this direction: \\citet{du2026geometric} informally observe that expected calibration error rises before accuracy degrades; SIGMA~\\cite{sigma2026spectral} proposes spectral indicators of distributional shift; and DCES~\\cite{dces2026counter} uses center-edge sampling dynamics as a collapse signal.\nYet the statistical methodology underlying these lead-lag claims (whether a canary metric genuinely \\emph{precedes} downstream degradation, or merely correlates with it under shared non-stationary trends) has received little scrutiny.\n\nWe identify several methodological deficiencies that pervade existing lead-lag analyses of iterative training collapse:\n(1)~no differencing tests to rule out spurious regression arising from shared non-stationary trends, a well-known hazard when correlating co-trending time series;\n(2)~no multi-method cross-validation; conclusions rest on a single statistical method whose implicit assumptions about stationarity and directionality are rarely examined;\n(3)~no multiple comparison correction (e.g., Benjamini--Hochberg FDR control), increasing the risk of false discoveries when testing many metric pairs simultaneously; and\n(4)~no regime-dependent analysis, despite the possibility that the same statistical test yields opposite conclusions at different contamination levels.\nThe closest predecessor is \\citet{du2026geometric}, who observe that calibration error rises before accuracy degrades in iteratively trained language models.\nTheir analysis, however, is limited to a single metric pair, spans only 1--3 generations, and reports informal visual comparisons without statistical tests, leaving open the question of whether the observed lead is genuine or an artifact of shared trends.\nThe early-warning framework of \\citet{scheffer2009early,scheffer2012anticipating}, widely adopted in ecology for anticipating tipping points, provides the theoretical motivation for canary-based prediction, but has not been systematically applied to iterative training collapse with the methodological rigor that short, noisy time series demand~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\n\nWe address these gaps through a systematic case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse, the progressive loss of lexical diversity in model-generated text across training generations.\nUsing Pythia-410M across five contamination levels ($\\alpha \\in \\{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\\}$) and up to four random seeds, we expose three methodological pitfalls that undermine naive lead-lag analysis:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Method sensitivity.} Applied to the same data (16 candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, including candidates later excluded; \\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), five lead-lag detection methods yield FDR significance rates ranging from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 25\\% (Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality), with inter-method agreement of Cohen's $\\kappa{=}0.185$ (poor agreement).\n    \\item \\textbf{Spurious regression.} Canary$\\to$perplexity associations appear near-universal in undifferenced analysis but vanish entirely after first-differencing ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), revealing them as artifacts of co-trending rather than genuine temporal precedence.\n    \\item \\textbf{Regime dependence.} Permutation Granger tests find 24/24 canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant at full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$) but 0/24 at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (three seeds), with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (0/8--6/8, three seeds), demonstrating that detectability depends sharply on both contamination level and training stochasticity.\n\\end{enumerate}\nTo move beyond single-method analysis, we propose a multi-method consensus framework with empirically calibrated thresholds, requiring agreement across multiple independent statistical methods and random seeds before classifying a lead-lag relationship as robust.\n\nThe framework's utility is demonstrated by its calibration on built-in experimental controls.\nNaive cross-correlation identifies 100\\% of canary--collapse metric pairs as FDR-significant; the consensus pipeline (requiring ${\\geq}4/5$ methods and ${\\geq}3/4$ seeds) retains only those pairs meeting effect-size criteria calibrated against the null control.\nOn reference extremes, the framework achieves a false positive rate of 0\\% at the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ null control (0/24 pairs, three seeds) (where individual methods reach up to 100\\% FPR) and a false negative rate of 0\\% against six effect-size reference pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nApplying the framework, we identify token entropy as a one-generation-ahead canary for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$: the full consensus pipeline confirms directional asymmetry with cross-correlation $|r|{>}0.99$, 24/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant, and 0/24 in the reverse direction.\nThree-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ extends this finding (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse via permutation Granger), though method-level consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3).\nThe onset boundary of causal detectability lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, below which all seeds agree on null.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality~\\cite{shojaie2022granger}.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic exposure of three methodological pitfalls} (method sensitivity, spurious regression, regime dependence) in lead-lag analysis of iterative training collapse.\n    Method choice alone inflates spurious-signal rates to at least 62.5\\%; first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely; and the same Granger test shifts from 24/24 FDR-significant pairs to 0/8 across contamination levels.\n    These results demonstrate that single-method, single-regime analyses, the current norm in this literature, are unreliable.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{A multi-method consensus framework} that functions as a diagnostic filter rather than a majority vote: the $K{\\geq}4/5$ threshold is the unique operating point where both FPR and FNR reach 0\\% on reference extremes ($K{=}3$: FPR${=}$37.5\\%; $K{=}5$: FNR${=}$50\\%).\n    Shapley-value decomposition of method contributions reveals that permutation Granger dominates the ensemble (Shapley${=}{+}0.375$) while Toda--Yamamoto contributes negatively; these diagnostics provide principled guidance for method panel selection.\n    The framework achieves FNR${=}$0\\% on effect-size reference pairs (4-method panel, permutation Granger power${=}1.0$) with four-tier practical guidance.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Identification of token entropy as a robust one-generation-ahead canary} for diversity collapse at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, confirmed by 4 independent methods, 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium, with complete directional asymmetry (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse); directional asymmetry extends to $\\alpha{=}0.75$ across 3~seeds (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse) in a transitional consensus regime (Tier~2--3). The claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-architecture generalization} (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): validation on GPT-2-medium (345M parameters) confirms that the entropy-leads-diversity pattern, the consensus framework's operating thresholds, and the detectability boundary at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$ all transfer across decoder-only transformer families, with all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reaching Tier~3 consensus at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ and 0/6 in the reverse direction.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows.\n\\S\\ref{sec:related_work} surveys related work on model collapse, early-warning signals, and lead-lag methodology.\n\\S\\ref{sec:background} establishes the formal setup, notation, and the five detection methods used throughout.\n\\S\\ref{sec:method} distills the three methodological pitfalls into a unified diagnostic framework, presents the multi-method consensus pipeline, and reports its empirical calibration.\n\\S\\ref{sec:experiments} provides full experimental results: method sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}), regime-dependent detectability (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}), a case study of canary prediction of diversity collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}), consensus framework validation (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}), and cross-architecture generalization (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications (including the practical utility paradox, the token entropy--perplexity distinction, and NLP-specific constraints on lead-lag analysis) and outlines directions for future work.\n\\S\\ref{sec:conclusion} concludes, followed by a dedicated Limitations section (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).",

1. method.tex L72-73 的旧值 "+0.375" 和 "−2.625" 必须删除（那是 LOO 值，且 −2.625 在 [0,1] 游戏上不可能存在，审稿人一看就知道有问题）
2. introduction.tex L45 如果引用了这些数字也必须更新
3. TY 的定性描述从 "contributes negatively" 改为 "is the weakest contributor to sensitivity (Shapley(FNR) = −0.158 vs −0.210 for other methods)"
4. 核心叙事保持：Naive XCorr 是 FPR 唯一正贡献者，PermG 是最强 FPR 抑制器
5. Figure caption 中注明参数：32 coalition 枚举，K=max(1,|S|−1)，FPR 基于 α=0.0 × 3 seeds × 8 pairs，FNR 基于 α=1.0 × 4 seeds × 6 reference pairs

如果你已经在用之前我给的数字（−0.110/−0.076/+0.192），全部替换为上面的 v3 值。无 em-dash。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YX6RGpkiMGHtNrkCUN4uEw`</summary>

```text
72	Shapley value analysis (exact enumeration of all 32 coalitions; Figure~\ref{fig:shapley}) identifies naive cross-correlation as the sole positive contributor to FPR (Shapley${=}{+}0.192$) and permutation Granger as the strongest FPR suppressor (Shapley${=}{-}0.110$), the only method combining 0\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.
73	Toda--Yamamoto's contribution to sensitivity is the weakest in the panel (Shapley${=}{-}0.033$ vs.\ ${-}0.242$ for the next weakest method); we retain it as insurance against unknown integration order, but note its severe power limitations at $T{\leq}10$.
74	
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/shapley_method_contribution.pdf}
78	\caption{Shapley value decomposition of method contributions to consensus FPR and FNR (exact enumeration of all $2^5{=}32$ coalitions, $K{=}\lceil 0.8|S|\rceil$ threshold). FPR is computed at $\alpha{=}0.0$ (3~seeds $\times$ 8~pairs${=}$24 observations); FNR at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, 6 reference pairs). Naive cross-correlation is the only positive FPR contributor (${+}0.192$); permutation Granger is the strongest FPR suppressor (${-}0.110$). Toda--Yamamoto contributes the least to sensitivity (${-}0.033$), consistent with its low power at $T{=}10$.}
79	\label{fig:shapley}
80	\end{figure}
81	Tier~4 requires unanimity across methods and seeds, which incurs a 50\% false negative rate because Toda--Yamamoto produces Type~II errors at $T{=}10$ due to insufficient residual degrees of freedom (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
```

</details>
